follows because Pm  0 K is finite rank and every compact operator is uniformly small after
                        T
applying I − Pm 0 . If the claimed essential-rate bound failed, choose unit u ∈ ran(I − P 0 )
                                                                               m            m
and unit inputs pm witnessing the excess. The outputs are weakly null, so the previous
diagonal construction applies to these arbitrary witnesses; approximate the inputs too by
finite exact test combinations while preserving the pairing. Split KT = KT −1 K1 for T > 1.
Theorem 8.1 supplies a uniform stationary Hessian budget for the exact inputs K1 pm . Since
their derivatives are independent of the differentiating spin, their wj -weighted budget equals
their vj -weighted budget. Apply Theorem 7.1 to the hidden outputs and these smoothed
inputs. Here the hidden theorem imports the multitime conditional derivative argument: its
fixed smooth circuits use the mixed-slot hierarchy, common tensor truncations, and opened
network reduction of Theorems 4.4, 4.5 and 5.12. Its path entry comparisons retain the typical
integrated-parameter convention of Theorem 5.1; no supremum over auxiliary Duhamel times
is added here. The bounded K1 factor and loss of one time unit disappear after dividing the
logarithm by T . This proves Equation (8.33) for all possible norm witnesses.
    The dense embedding and its spectral projections. Fix 0 < ρ < λsp and choose T so large
that the essential norm of TX := KT |X is smaller than e−ρT . Choose r > e−ρT away from
the spectrum, with r < 1. The usual resolvent argument for a finite-rank perturbation of an
operator of norm smaller than r gives a Riesz decomposition

                                         X = Er ⊕ Fr ,

where Er is finite dimensional, contains the spectrum outside |z| = r, and the spectral radius
of TX |Fr is at most r. For clarity, a finite-rank approximation with smaller remainder norm
makes the resolvent outside that norm the inverse of a finite-dimensional analytic matrix;
its zeros are isolated and have finite multiplicity. On a compact annulus |z| ≥ r there are
finitely many such zeros. This supplies precisely the decomposition just used.
    Let Tv = KT |Xv . It is positive self-adjoint and ιTX = Tv ι. Injectivity implies that every
generalized eigenvector in Er embeds as a generalized eigenvector of Tv . Self-adjointness
removes all nontrivial Jordan chains and forces these eigenvalues to be real and positive.
Write Wr = ιEr . For f ∈ Fr and r′ > r, the spectral-radius formula yields
                                ⃦      ⃦
                                ⃦Tv ιf ⃦ ≤ ∥ι∥ Cr′ (r ′ )k ∥f ∥0 .
                                ⃦ k ⃦
                                        v

If the Tv -spectral measure of ιf charged (r′ , ∞), its kth moment in this inequality would
grow faster than (r′ )k . The spectral theorem therefore forces that measure into [0, r′ ]; let
r′ ↓ r. Thus ιFr is orthogonal to Wr . On the dense image ιX the Riesz decomposition is
already an orthogonal decomposition into Wr and the low spectral subspace. It follows that

                             Xv = Wr ⊕⊥ ιFr ,         ΠWr ι = ιPEr .

Density now excludes additional weighted spectral mass outside [0, r]. The displayed identity
proves agreement of the projections through ι; no boundedness of an ordinary Riesz projection
in the weaker norm was assumed.
    Given any rate strictly below λsp , choose ρ above that rate and then r between the
essential disk and its corresponding semigroup eigenvalue. The preceding argument accounts

                                                 95
for all weighted spectrum below that rate by finitely many eigenvectors in X . If the bottom
λ is strictly below λsp , it is therefore attained and isolated. Its complement has weighted
generator spectrum bounded below by λ + η for some η > 0. The inverse Ev → E is bounded
because these spaces are finite dimensional, so its composition with Πv ι gives the asserted
projection for vectors of H whose image is weighted-exact. Applying Theorem 8.12 to their
complementary parts proves the last assertion.

Remark 8.14. The construction of λ uses stationary fixed-time limits alone. The subsequent
use of Theorem 8.11 on a nonstationary trajectory chooses a fixed interior smoothing interval,
a finite set of recipes, derivative and likelihood truncations, and the required conditional
simulation accuracies before dimension. Only after those limits are established are the finite
bases enlarged and spectral or large-block limits taken. This order permits arbitrary scalar
coefficients in Equation (8.11) and keeps the nonequivalent Hilbert norms distinct throughout.


9     From fixed blocks to cutoff
We now convert the fixed-time spectral estimates into cutoff. Uniform contraction on one
sufficiently long fixed block gives the upper bound. For the lower bound, we use a linear
statistic if the rate is the spin edge, and prepare a slow visible mode otherwise. Throughout,
0 < β < 1 is fixed, λ is the deterministic number in Equation (8.6), and Euclidean norms
remain unnormalized. For a forward trajectory (Xs ) and a terminal function f at time T ,
write
                   ps (x) = dST −s f (x),   Ns2 = E ∥ps (Xs )∥2 ,   0 ≤ s ≤ T.
Thus p evolves backwards while the law in the expectation evolves forwards. Bounds stated
uniformly for polynomial tests mean that, for each fixed M < ∞, they hold for every real
f with ∥f ∥∞ ≤ nM . Increasing M by a fixed amount is always allowed before selecting the
good-disorder event. In particular, normalization by a norm at least an inverse power of n
preserves this class.

9.1   Uniform blocks and deterministic selection
Restarting on a fixed interval. The limiting statements of Section 8 use prescribed
comparison times. We will apply them to blocks whose positions may depend on the dimension
and the disorder. The following finite-chain identities put such a block on a fixed relative
interval before any limit is taken. Fix its length ℓ > 0. Conditional on J, let the original
chain start from ν, and choose 0 ≤ a ≤ T − ℓ and a terminal function f . These choices may
depend on n, J, but not on the realized trajectory. Set

    We will use ℓ = D or 2D for a fixed block length D. The endpoints 0, D (and 2D when
ℓ = 2D) can then be retained as prescribed times in Theorem 8.5. Interior comparison times
are still selected using Theorem 8.6 and the integrated comparison bounds. The restart does
not replace that selection by a supremum over exceptional auxiliary times.
Proposition 9.1 (Uniform contraction on a fixed block). Fix 0 < ξ < λ, a polynomial
envelope M , a horizon constant A < ∞, and H < ∞. There are deterministic constants
D, b0 < ∞ and disorder events of probability tending to one on which the following assertion
holds simultaneously for all starting configurations, all such terminal functions, and all
b0 ≤ a < a + D ≤ T ≤ A log n:

                                 Na2 ≤ e−2(λ−ξ)D Na+D
                                                  2
                                                      + n−H .                                (9.3)

The same assertion, with no burn-in, holds in the deterministic typicality convention of
Theorem 5.1 for families of initial laws satisfying log ∥ dν/ dµ∥∞ = o(n) uniformly. The
constants may be chosen after H and M ; no quantitative rate for a fixed-time o(1) is asserted
or required.
Proof. The proof has three steps: a limiting contraction with strict slack, uniformity down
to an inverse-polynomial norm, and a fixed burn-in. Begin with a block whose initial law
has vanishing specific log density. Work first with a fixed D > 2, which will be enlarged
below. For each possible choice of its origin and terminal horizon, apply Equation (9.1) with
ℓ = D and terminal function g = ST −a−D f . In the limiting argument we use these restarted
objects, denoting the process again by X, so that a = 0, T = D, and the endpoint times are
prescribed. Normalize ND = 1. The rough square estimate in Theorem 4.2, integrated over
the last unit of this interval, supplies a time s ∈ [D − 1, D − 1/2] at which

                                E ∥ps ∥2 + E        wj ∥dj ps ∥2 ≤ C.
                                               ∑︂
                                                                                             (9.4)
                                               j

One can impose the reverse Hellinger estimate of Theorem 8.6 at the same time. Both
requirements hold on a set of times of positive measure after enlarging their fixed constants.

                                                   97
By Theorem 8.11, the visible projection qs therefore lies in the exact weighted sector, including
its scalar Hilbert factor. By Theorems 8.3, 8.5 and 8.12, its propagation to the prescribed
time 0 has norm at most
                                   Cρ e−ρ(D−1) ,      ρ < λ.
Conditional expectation on the scalar factor is a contraction. The upgrade applies to an
unweighted visible vector whose weighted image lies in Xv ; it does not require membership in
X . The hidden remainder at time 0 satisfies the corresponding estimate with any rate strictly
below λsp , by Theorem 7.1, testing against that remainder itself. Finite recipe projections
followed by increasing the projection rank justify this test; the norm and Hessian bounds in
Equation (9.4) are exactly its input hypotheses. Consequently, for every ρ < λ,

                                   lim sup N0 ≤ Cρ e−ρ(D−1) .                               (9.5)

Choose ρ ∈ (λ − ξ, λ) and then D so large that the right side is strictly smaller than e−(λ−ξ)D .
This leaves fixed multiplicative slack.
    We specify how the comparison time is selected, since the path estimates also contain
auxiliary integration variables. First impose finitely many recipe, conditional-pair, and matrix
tests at a fixed accuracy. The exponential stationary discard bounds transfer through the
upper density bound and every fixed polynomial weight. Bounds for deterministic masks
are integrated over their simplex variables before this transfer. Fubini and extraction give
convergence in measure on the bounded domains of auxiliary times. The uniform rough
L2 estimates, with the Hessian budget when it occurs, control the resulting integrals. In
particular, we do not bound an exceptional time integral by its Lebesgue measure times
a polynomial pointwise supremum. Increasing the finite test lists and decreasing their
accuracies permits one common choice of s along a subsequence. Strong continuity, or a
further fixed positive smoothing time, treats a convergent sequence of selected times. This is
the deterministic countable-test content of Theorem 5.1.
    To obtain the additive accuracy in Equation (9.3), consider an arbitrary proposed violating
sequence of starts, functions, origins an , and horizons Tn . Restart each at an on [0, D], with
gn = STn −an −D fn , as above, and set B = ND  2 for its restarted endpoint norm. If


                                        B ≤ e−CD n−H ,
                                                                                         √
the rough estimate already gives N02 ≤ n−H . Otherwise divide p and its potential by B. A
polynomial pointwise bound remains, since B −1/2 ≤ eCD/2 nH/2 . If the claimed inequality
failed along a sequence of deterministic good disorders, its normalized version would contradict
the strict slack in Equation (9.5). Indeed the restarted laws satisfy Equation (9.2), their
normalized terminal functions remain polynomially bounded, and every endpoint comparison
is now at 0 or D. Only the interior good time is selected after imposing the integrated
tests. Equation (9.1) then returns the estimate to the original block, regardless of the
sequences an , Tn . This proves the required uniformity. The inverse-power remainder comes
from normalization and strict slack, rather than a rate assigned to a fixed-time o(1).
    We finally prove that one fixed burn-in works with disorder probability tending to one.
A deterministic diagonal contradiction will place every required typicality test on the same
sequence of disorders; convergence in probability of the limiting block estimate alone would
not supply this conclusion. Enumerate the deterministic good-disorder inequalities just used,
including the quenched exponential bounds for stationary exceptional sets and the uniform
forward superpolynomial discards. For the first k tests, with their fixed tolerances, let their
positive exponential rates be c1 , . . . , ck . By Theorem 3.5, choose fixed numbers bk ↑ ∞ so
that                                       2/3

therefore contradicts the same fixed contraction slack. No lower bound on the sequence εk
was used. Thus some fixed b0 works, as asserted.

the initial contribution is at most C0 ν ∥dSt f ∥2 . The exponential integral is at most 1/(2ρ).
Apply Equation (9.6) with an accuracy two powers better than the displayed one to absorb
the time factor. Thus the constant in Equation (9.9) is fixed independently of the test’s
particular polynomial normalization.

                                                   101
9.3   Alignment with an isolated visible eigenspace
Assume henceforth that λ < λsp . Theorem 8.13 supplies a finite-dimensional slow eigenspace.
Write E = (E1 , . . . , Em ) for a basis orthonormal in the weighted metric. Its columns have
finite unweighted norm; let CE bound the norm of the embedding c ↦→ Ec into that space.
The projection onto this eigenspace is the same under the weighted spectral decomposition
and the unweighted isolated spectral decomposition. All its complements needed below decay
at a rate strictly greater than λ.
    We approximate the slow columns twice, for different purposes. Coarse smooth recipes
B control coefficient fluctuations; their derivative constants are fixed before choosing the
alignment block. Fine recipes E      ˜︁ then report the coefficients and define the preparation.
Keeping these choices separate prevents the finer derivative constants from changing the
block length that has already been chosen.
Proposition 9.6 (Mean and fluctuation alignment). Fix a logarithmic horizon, a polynomial
envelope, and a common constant CP in the defective trajectory Poincaré bound Equation (9.9),
with arbitrarily prescribed inverse-power accuracy. Consider uniformly typically initialized
families of ordinary trajectories satisfying this bound. For every η > 0, one can choose a
fixed block length D and fixed smooth recipes E ˜︁ such that, on disorder events of probability
tending to one, the following holds uniformly. Put
                                       ˜︁ r )t D
                                        [︁                      ]︁

For a = b − D, suppose Nb ≥ n−Q , where Q is any exponent fixed in advance. Suppose also
that either pb is a fixed nondegenerate finite linear combination of concentrating visible exact
test gradients, or there is a subsequent block with Nb+D ≤ RNb for a fixed finite R. Then
                                        (︁ ⃦           ⃦2 )︁

The ratio R is used only to establish exactness at b; it does not enter the choice of D or the
fluctuation estimate.
Proof. We may choose D > 2. First put the block at prescribed relative times using
Equation (9.1). In the terminal alternative take ℓ = D and g = ST −b f . In the subsequent-
block alternative take ℓ = 2D and g = ST −b−D f . In both cases the restart origin is a = b − D.
The new initial law is the old marginal at a, with the same upper density bound and common
trajectory Poincaré constant. The gradient and recipe contractions are unchanged at matching
times; in particular Aˆ︁r = Aa+r . The terminal alternative is the same condition on pˆ︁D , while
the other alternative gives N   ˆ︁2D ≤ RNˆ︁D . For the limiting argument denote the restarted
process again by X and use a = 0, b = D; retain also 2D in the second alternative. Normalize
ND = 1.
     We first establish exactness at D, then control the mean and fluctuation of the slow coef-
ficient, and finally recover a uniform finite-dimensional estimate. In the terminal alternative,
qb lies in the visible exact sector by assumption. In the subsequent-block alternative, the
budget N2D ≤ R allows Theorem 8.11 at a good time selected in a fixed subinterval compactly
contained in (D, 2D). Both adjacent time separations thus stay positive after extraction.
Transport back to the prescribed time D by Theorem 8.5 gives the same conclusion at b = D.
The value of the finite budget R has served this purpose and is not used again.
     Retain 0, D and the good time s ∈ [D − 1, D − 1/2] in the same scalar extraction, together

    The block length is now fixed, so we may choose the finer recipes E˜︁ without altering the
fluctuation estimate. At this D, an accuracy smaller than cηe  −(λ+C)D   makes all changes in
the reported Aa , Ab , and the reconstruction smaller than the remaining fraction of ηe−λD .
Here eCD is a rough bound for any endpoint norm needed in this comparison. The finer
derivative constants have not replaced CB in Equation (9.19).
    Finally use strict slack, the deterministic good-disorder selection in the proof of Theo-
rem 9.1, and normalization by Nb . The condition Nb ≥ n−Q preserves a fixed polynomial
envelope; choose every Poincaré and propagation remainder with an exponent larger than
2Q + H + 2 before using it. For a violating sequence, perform the one-block or two-block
restart above before extraction. The normalized gradients, actual conditional laws, common
Poincaré bound, and endpoint ratio meet the same hypotheses at the prescribed relative
endpoints; any parameter selected from the family is fixed conditional on the disorder before
the path is sampled. The resulting fixed-interval sequence would contradict the limiting
estimate. Returning by Equation (9.1) proves Equation (9.16) uniformly at the original block
positions, without a rate for any fixed-time o(1). This includes the parameter selected from
the preparation family below.

Lemma 9.10 (Lower cutoff for an isolated visible rate). If λ < λsp , the lower conclusion of
Theorem 9.5 still holds.

Proof. Fix 0 < ϵ < 1, take 0 < α < ϵ/8 and 0 < ξ1 < λϵ/4, and round (1 − ϵ)tn up to an

                              che−(λ+ξ1 )D n 1/2−α−(1−ϵ)(λ+ξ1 )/(2λ) .

The exponent is positive, so the ball of attainable means has diverging radius. In particular,
one may choose a preparation for which the mean of F1 differs from µF1 by a quantity tending
to infinity. The variances of F1 under νz St and µ are bounded: use the common trajectory
Poincaré estimate and its bounded pointwise gradient for the first, and the equilibrium gap
for the second. Chebyshev’s inequality implies ∥νz St − µ∥TV → 1. Since νz is a probability
law on starting configurations, convexity bounds this distance by supx ∥St (x, ·) − µ∥TV .
Monotonicity then gives the same lower bound at the unrounded, earlier time (1 − ϵ)tn .

Theorem 9.11 (Continuous-time cutoff). For every fixed 0 < β < 1 and ϵ ∈ (0, 1), put
tn = log n/(2λ). Then       ⃦                   ⃦
                        sup ⃦S(1−ϵ)tn (x, ·) − µ⃦ −→ 1,
                            ⃦                   ⃦
                         x                       TV
                            ⃦                   ⃦                             (9.30)
                        sup ⃦S(1+ϵ)tn (x, ·) − µ⃦ −→ 0,
                            ⃦                   ⃦
                                 x                         TV

in probability over the disorder. The number λ > 0 is given entirely by the dimension-first
equilibrium construction in Equation (8.6).

Proof. The upper bound follows from Theorem 9.4. For the lower bound, Theorem 9.5 applies
when λ = λsp , and Theorem 9.10 applies when λ < λsp . These cases exhaust the definition of
λ.

    Theorem 2.4 supplies the continuous-time conclusion at β = 0, with λ(0) = 1. It remains
to transfer both cases to the discrete update clock.

9.5   The discrete update clock
Proposition 9.12 (Cutoff for single-site update attempts). Let P = I + L/n, so one step
selects a uniform site and refreshes its spin, including attempts that leave the state unchanged.
For every fixed 0 ≤ β < 1, its worst-case total-variation cutoff is at
                                             n log n
                                                     ,
                                             2λ(β)

in probability over the disorder.




                                                  108
Proof. We prove both directions of clock transfer. Write dc (t) and dd (k) for the continuous
and discrete distances; their kernels satisfy the exact Poissonization identity St = ent(P −I) .
For k = ⌊(1 − ϵ)ntn ⌋ and T = (1 − ϵ/2)tn , convexity and discrete-time contraction give

                            dc (T ) ≤ P{Poisson(nT ) < k} + dd (k).

The probability is O((ntn )−1 ), while dc (T ) → 1, proving the discrete lower bound.
   For the upper bound, a uniform holding probability permits the local Poisson-to-binomial
lemma. Restrict to the high-probability event ∥J∥op ≤ K. At every configuration,
                                                                                      √

                           P = (1 − ϑ)I + ϑR,        St = eϑnt(R−I) .

Here R is a Markov kernel preserving µ. Set M = ⌈(1 + ϵ)ntn ⌉. For every x and |f | ≤ 1, put

                       EuPoisson(ϑM +y√M ) = SM/n+y√M /(ϑn) f (x) − µf.

The time correction is Oy ( log n/n), and hence this time exceeds (1 + ϵ/2)tn for large n.
The absolute value of the display is bounded by 2dc ((1 + ϵ/2)tn ) → 0, uniformly over x, f .
    We have verified every shifted Poisson average required by Theorem 2.3, uniformly over
starts and tests. Apply that uniform transfer: its assumptions are a fixed ϑ ∈ (0, 1), M → ∞,
bounded arrays, and vanishing Poisson averages for every fixed shift y, all just verified. It
gives
                           EuBinomial(M,ϑ) = P M f (x) − µf −→ 0
uniformly over the test family. Taking half its supremum proves dd (M ) → 0. For convergence
in disorder probability, the proof of that lemma uses a fixed finite signed approximation by
shifted Gaussian densities; its finitely many Poisson averages are all bounded by the same
random quantity 2dc ((1 + ϵ/2)tn ). Thus it applies on the present high-probability events
without any additional convergence rate.


A     An independent gradient argument below one half
These appendices prove cutoff for 0 ≤ β < 1/2 by following the unweighted gradient along the
heat-bath dynamics. The proof places the median mixing time on the logarithmic scale and
shows that the total-variation transition has vanishing relative width. The central estimate is
pointwise: the gradient dissipates at a fixed positive rate outside a set of exponentially small
Gibbs mass. Entropy decay first makes that exceptional set unlikely, and smoothing over a
positive logarithmic lag then converts gradient control into the cutoff ratio.
    This argument also yields estimates with uses beyond its cutoff conclusion. They include
a spectral-gap and covariance bound uniform over external fields and interaction scalings, and
the scalar variational bound 0.9969 with its complete analytic and arithmetic certificate. The
proof is independent of the limiting exact-gradient operator used in the full high-temperature
argument.
    Keep the model, half-differences, and two clock conventions of Section 2. Write PJ
for the interaction-matrix law and abbreviate the worst-start total-variation distances by

                                              109
dc (t) = dn (t) and dd (k) = ddisc
                              n (k). We will use the two gradient densities and the median
mixing time
                  ∑︂                           ∑︂
       U (f ) =       (di f )2 ,   Γ(f ) =          wi (di f )2 ,   tmed
                                                                     n   = inf{t ≥ 0 : dc (t) ≤ 1/2}.
                  i                             i

The weights are wi = 1 − xi tanh((Jx)i ). Expanding the square at each spin flip gives the
identity
                                 L(f 2 ) − 2f Lf = 2Γ(f )                            (A.1)
                                                                                    ∫︁
For a probability measure ν and an integrable function f , write νf = f dν. All vector and
matrix norms in these appendices are Euclidean and Euclidean operator norms, respectively,
unless explicitly specified. We continue to write Px and Ex for chain probabilities and
expectations after fixing the disorder.
Theorem A.1. For every fixed β ∈ [0, 1/2), ε ∈ (0, 1/2), and η > 0, let

                                   tn,β,J (ε) = min{k ≥ 0 : dd (k) ≤ ε}.

Then                                    {︄                          }︄
                                               tn,β,J (ε)
                                   PJ                       >1+η         −→ 0.
                                             tn,β,J (1 − ε)
The denominator is positive for sufficiently large n.
    The denominator is eventually positive because dd (0) = 1 − minx µ(x) ≥ 1 − 2−n . We
prove the stated ratio directly; the full-phase theorem is not an input to this appendix
argument.
    The proof has three stages. Section B starts with a uniform two-spin inequality, obtains
the gap under all external fields and interaction scalings, and passes from that gap to entropy
decay by Gaussian observations. Section C expresses the pointwise gradient dissipation as a
matrix inequality. The matrix comparison in Section D and the scalar estimate in Section E
establish that inequality outside an exponentially small Gibbs set. Finally, Sections F and G
use concentration and positive-lag smoothing to prove the cutoff ratio, invoking the general
jump-moment and clock lemmas stated in Section 2. Section H supplies every quadrature
and arithmetic enclosure used in the scalar estimate.
    The temperature parameter β remains fixed throughout, and constants may depend on it.
Whenever an estimate is used on a logarithmic time horizon, its times are at most D∗ log n,
with D∗ < ∞ chosen before taking n → ∞.


B      Uniform spectral and entropy estimates
The entropy estimate needed for the independent cutoff argument rests on a spectral gap that
survives both changes of external field and reductions of the interaction strength. We prove
this gap by summing a signed two-spin inequality, then pass to entropy through Gaussian
observations. The observation posterior changes both the field and the interaction, which is
why both uniformities are needed. Throughout this section, the inverse temperature β < 1/2
is fixed.

B.1    Gaussian estimates and a good disorder event
We first collect the Gaussian tools used to control the operator norm, the row-square sums,
and the largest entry of the interaction matrix. These three controls will make the two-spin
error summable on a single disorder event.

    The signed two-spin estimate below follows the gap method of Wang [26, Theorem 1.2
and Lemma 3.4]. Keeping the sign of the leading interaction term allows the later summation
to use an operator norm. We give the algebra and the remainder estimate that make this
summation uniform in the external fields and the interaction scale.

B.3    Entropy through Gaussian observations
We now turn the uniform covariance estimate into entropy dissipation. For a probability
measure ν and a positive function f , the homogeneous entropy functional is

                               Entν (f ) = ν[f log f ] − ν[f ] log ν[f ].

The convention 0 log 0 = 0 extends it to nonnegative functions. Proposition B.5 imposes no
normalization on f . The proof uses the Gaussian observation entropy factorization principle
of Chen and Eldan [10, Theorem 49 in arXiv:2203.04163v2]. We include the argument to
keep the interaction scaling and the rate-one-per-site clock explicit.

Proposition B.5 (Entropy inequality and reduction from point starts). There are constants
C1 , C2 < ∞, depending only on the fixed β < 1/2, such that for all sufficiently large n and
J ∈ En , the zero-field Gibbs measure satisfies
                                                [︁                     ]︁
                            Entµ (f ) ≤ C1 µ f (−L) log f ,                  f > 0.     (B.6)

For every initial point x and every t ≥ 0, if ρt,x = St (x, ·), then

                                               dρt,x
                                          (︃           )︃
                                   Entµ                     ≤ C2 ne−t/C1 .              (B.7)
                                                dµ
In particular, the continuous-time worst-start mixing time at every fixed positive accuracy is
Oβ (log n), uniformly on these events.

Proof. We interpolate from the Gibbs measure to product posteriors, compare their expected
entropy with the initial entropy, and then remove the observation from the one-site terms.
For large n, Equation (B.2) ensures ∥J∥ < 1. Choose K = J + 2I; then K is positive definite
and satisfies ∥K∥ < 3. Keep it fixed throughout the argument for the realized disorder. Given
X ∼ µ and an independent standard Gaussian vector z, use the observation, for 0 < u ≤ 1,
                                                    √
                                    Yu = uK 1/2 X + u z.

Discarding the diagonal term, which is constant on the cube, leaves interaction matrix (1−u)J.
The observation creates an external field, and a further linear tilt changes only that field.

covariance norm at most C = κ−1

Differentiation and integration by parts are legitimate: the spin sums are finite, f is bounded

finite space, H(u) → Entµ (f ), giving the initial value for the comparison.
Covariance controlled by entropy. We next bound the derivative by the expected entropy
itself. Let ν be any posterior, take a unit vector a, and set Z = aT (X − ν[X]). The second
derivative of the log moment-generating function of Z is the variance of aT X under a linear
tilt of ν. Applying the uniform covariance estimate to these tilts and integrating twice gives

We have now compared the initial entropy with the entropy at the product endpoint. The
comparison used covariance control at every interaction scale: controlling linear tilts only at
the original interaction J would leave the quadratic change in Equation (B.8) untreated. It
remains to express the endpoint entropy in terms of the original one-site energies.
Removing the observation at the product endpoint. For a product probability measure
   ⨂︁
π = i πi , entropy tensorizes:
                                                   ∑︂
                                    Entπ (f ) ≤         π[Entπi (f )],
                                                    i

where in the ith summand the other coordinates are fixed inside the one-coordinate entropy.
To prove tensorization, separate one coordinate using the entropy chain rule and apply
induction to the remaining product. For a fixed base measure, f ↦→ Ent(f ) is convex; hence
averaging the separated coordinate can only decrease the remaining one-coordinate entropies.
The required convexity follows from joint convexity of (a, b) ↦→ a log(a/b) with a = f (x) and
b = π[f ].
    Apply tensorization to the product posterior at u = 1. We must then average under the
joint law of (X, Y1 ), retaining the dependence between the observation and the spins. For
this step put F = f (X), φ(z) = z log z,

        Gi = σ(X−i ),       Hi = σ(X−i , Y1 ),            eA (F ) = E[φ(F ) | A] − φ(E[F | A]).

Conditional Jensen and the tower property give

                     E[eHi (F ) | Gi ] = E[φ(F ) | Gi ] − E[φ(E[F | Hi ]) | Gi ]
                                     ≤ E[φ(F ) | Gi ] − φ(E[F | Gi ]) = eGi (F ).

Thus the additional conditioning on the observation decreases the averaged conditional entropy,
even though the observation and the complementary spins are dependent. Combining this
fact with the posterior tensorization gives
                           ∑︂                 ∑︂                 ∑︂ [︁                   ]︁
                 H(1) ≤         EeHi (F ) ≤        EeGi (F ) =        µ Entµ(·|X−i ) (f ) .
                            i                 i                   i

We can now compare each original one-coordinate entropy with the corresponding heat-bath
energy. Concavity of the logarithm gives

                                      Ent(f ) ≤ Cov(f, log f ),

because the difference of the right and left sides is Ef [log Ef − E log f ] ≥ 0. Moreover,
summing these conditional covariances gives
                ∑︂                                 ∑︂
                     µ[Cov(f, log f | X−i )] =          µ[f Di log f ] = µ[f (−L) log f ].
                 i                                  i

The product-endpoint estimate is therefore bounded by the entropy dissipation of the original
generator. Substituting into Equation (B.11) proves Equation (B.6), with the permissible
choice C1 = e3/κβ .

Reversibility lets us evolve the density ft = dρt,x /dµ using the same semigroup St . For t > 0

                      d

                      dt

    To finish, coarsen the measure to an event and its complement; relative entropy then
dominates the resulting binary relative entropy. For fixed q ∈ (0, 1), this binary entropy,
viewed as a function of p, has value and derivative zero at p = q and second derivative
1/[p(1 − p)] ≥ 4. It is consequently at least 2(p − q)2 . Choosing an event that attains total
variation and using the entropy decay just proved yields

which proves the stated mixing upper bound.


C     Dissipation of the unweighted gradient
The next step is a pointwise dissipation bound for the unweighted gradient U , with a fixed
contraction rate outside a set of exponentially small Gibbs mass. We first perform the
deterministic calculation: completing squares removes the second differences and leaves a
quadratic form in the first differences. The error estimate retains the variance factors needed
when local fields are large. We then apply the probability bounds proved in Sections D and E
to identify the exceptional set.
    Two row sums will control, respectively, the quadratic terms and the cubic error. For a
symmetric matrix J with zero diagonal, define
                                        ∑︂                                    ∑︂
                      R2 (J) = max            Jij2 ,       R3 (J) = max            |Jij |3 .
                                   i                                      i
                                          j                                    j

We begin at the all-positive configuration 1. In the quadratic form below all local quantities
are evaluated there: hi = (J1)i , mi = tanh hi , vi = 1 − m2i , and wi = 1 − mi . Define
                                   ∑︂                        ∑︂
                        MJ (p) =          Jij pi vj pj + 2        Jij2 pi mj vj pj

                                        ∑︂ Jij (vj pi + vi pj )2
                                            2                                                                 (C.1)
                                   +                                 ,        p ∈ Rn .
                                        i<j
                                                       wi + w j

For a finite matrix J, every denominator is positive. The estimates below remain uniform as
any local field tends to infinity, including when these denominators approach zero.
   To transfer the calculation to an arbitrary configuration x, use the sign change
         J x = diag(x)J diag(x),          f x (y) = f (x1 y1 , . . . , xn yn ),          pxi = xi di f (x).
This sign change conjugates the generator for J to the generator for J x . It also gives
di f x (1) = pxi and U (f x )(1) = U (f )(x), while preserving the matrix controls: ∥J x ∥ = ∥J∥,
Rk (J x ) = Rk (J) for k = 2, 3. The calculation at 1 therefore suffices for every configuration.

The constants depend only on the fixed β. If 0 ≤ β ≤ 7/20, the exceptional set can be empty.
Proof. We now combine the deterministic calculation with the disorder estimates. For the
smaller temperatures, the rough bound already contracts at every configuration. For the
remaining temperatures, the planted comparison will provide contraction outside a small
Gibbs set. Begin on the events of Proposition B.2, where the deterministic tolerances give
                          ∥J∥ ≤ 2β + o(1),        R2 (J) ≤ β 2 + o(1),
                              R3 (J) ≤ max |Jij | R2 (J) = o(1).

Sign conjugation preserves each bound, so they hold for every J x . The remainder in Lemma C.1

    If β ≤ 7/20, it suffices to substitute the disorder estimates into Equation (C.7). Its
coefficient is at most
                                 4    32                 189

The rational bound follows from 4/(3 3) + 32/27 < 2 at β = 7/20, since both terms increase
with nonnegative β. There is therefore a fixed margin below one. Absorbing the vanishing
remainder in Lemma C.1 proves Equation (C.8) with a fixed positive c and BJ = ∅ for
all sufficiently large n. The endpoint β = 0 can also be read directly from Equation (C.2):
aij = 0, wi = 1, and (∂t − L)U ≤ −2U . This part of the proof therefore uses no nondegenerate
Gaussian field law.
    For 7/20 < β < 1/2, we need a sharper estimate on most configurations. The inputs are
proved in Sections D and E, independently of Proposition C.2. First, Proposition E.1 bounds
the normalized variational expression in Equation (D.5) by 0.9969. Choose δ = 0.0031 and
ρ = δ/2 to retain half this strict margin. Proposition D.1 then gives κ > 0 for which the

D     A planted Gaussian comparison
We seek a bound on the quadratic form in Equation (C.1) outside a set of configurations of
exponentially small Gibbs mass. A bound of the form MJ x (p) < ∥p∥22 need not hold for every
x and every nonzero p. We therefore first fix a configuration and weight the disorder by its
unnormalized Gibbs weight. Under this planted law, a scalar variational estimate controls
the form simultaneously on all unit vectors. The final step transfers the resulting exponential
probability bound to Gibbs mass under the original disorder law.

D.1    The planted law and the variational bound

     The proof separates the squared interactions from the centered Gaussian matrix.
Lemma D.2 replaces the squares by their means without discarding the dependence between
fields and couplings. After this replacement, a finite partition of the fields reduces the matrix
term to the conditional comparison in Lemma D.3. A net of the normalized bin parameters
then gives simultaneous control of all unit vectors. We choose every approximation accuracy
before sending n to infinity, which retains the exponential failure rate required by the
proposition.

Proof. We first establish the unweighted operator estimate, then insert the continuous kernel

For uniform dominated convergence, use |βZ + β 2 / n| ≤ β|Z| + β 2 ; the square of the right
side has an exponential moment for a sufficiently small parameter. We may therefore choose
T so that both the mean tail and the logarithm of the displayed moment are as small as
required. Exponential Markov bounds the probability that a row average of (nJij2 − T )+
exceeds the prescribed accuracy by e−cn . Taking the union over the n rows retains a positive
exponential rate. Symmetry and entrywise nonnegativity bound the tail matrix’s operator
norm by its largest row sum, and the same estimate controls its expectation.
    For a fixed unit vector, the quadratic form of the centered truncated matrix is a sum
of independent bounded variables with coefficients 2pi pj /n, i < j. To bound its moment
generating function, recall that a centered variable whose range has length a has logarithmic
moment generating function at most a2 s2 /8. The second derivative is a variance under a
tilted law and is at most a2 /4; integrating twice gives this bound. Since i<j (2pi pj )2 ≤ 2,
                                                                           ∑︁

the present quadratic form has logarithmic moment generating function at most CT 2 s2 /n2 ,

volumes of disjoint balls of radius 1/8. For a symmetric matrix A, twice the maximum of
|pT Ap| on this net bounds ∥A∥. The union bound is therefore absorbed by the quadratic
exponential rate. It remains to restore the mean and diagonal:

D.4       Finite field bins and normalized parameters
The reduced form still depends on all n field values. We next replace them by a fixed
finite collection while retaining uniformity over every unit vector. Choose a finite partition
I = (I1 , . . . , Ik ) of the real line into intervals, with the two extreme intervals unbounded, and
choose a representative ha ∈ Ia for each interval. Write

             hI (h) = ha   (h ∈ Ia ),        ma = m(ha ),      va = v(ha ),       wa = w(ha ).

For every prescribed accuracy, the partition and representatives can be chosen so that
∥h − hI ∥L2 (Gβ ) is small, and so that m, v and the kernels in Equation (D.4) change uniformly
by a small amount on replacing each field by its representative. Construct the partition by
first choosing R so that the Gaussian square tails outside [−R, R] are small. In each tail
take a representative far enough out that tanh is uniformly close to its endpoint; uniform
continuity on the compactified w-square then controls both kernels. A fine mesh of [−R, R]
gives the remaining approximations. Choose every interval to have positive Gβ -probability.
    Let

We verify the exponential rates because the final comparison needs more than convergence
in probability. In the representation (D.13), the common shift lies in any prescribed small
fixed interval except with probability e−cn . For the independent fields Yi = β 2 + βZi , the
bounded-variable moment generating function estimate from the square-replacement proof
gives exponential concentration of each bin count. A common shift of magnitude at most
a can move a field to a different bin only when Yi is within a of a boundary. There are
finitely many boundaries. Choose a so that their neighborhoods have small total probability,
and concentrate their empirical count by the same estimate. For the empirical square error,
the mesh, common shift and boundary counts control the bounded middle interval. The
tail contribution is at most a constant times the empirical average of (1 + Yi2 )1{|Yi |>R−a} .
These variables possess an exponential moment at a small fixed parameter. Increasing R
makes its logarithm arbitrarily small, by the same domination argument used for Gaussian
square tails. Exponential Markov therefore makes the tail average small with probability
1−e−cn . The remaining assertion follows from H −β 2 ∼ N (0, 2β 2 /n). Thus every tolerance in
Equation (D.19) has a fixed positive exponential rate. These events also bound the empirical
second moment of H and ensure, for large n, at least two sites in every bin.

conditions in Equation (D.20).

   Concentration must, however, include Cb . For a unit vector p, the full GOE functional

                                                               131
    The comparison so far fixes b and r. We must now make it simultaneous over these
parameters. Although Cb has mean zero for each fixed b, optimizing b need not preserve
that property. We therefore use concentration for the full supremum just proved, and pass
through a finite net of the normalized parameters.
    Fix a mesh size η > 0. The feasible set in Equation (D.21) has an η-net inside that
set of size at most (1 + 2/η)2k , with an inessential enlargement of this bound if necessary.
Conditional on H, this net is fixed independently of W . At each net point, adding the
deterministic A to the full supremum and applying Lemma B.1 gives an upper bound

                                         Vβ,νˆ︁I (b, r) + a                               (D.30)

outside probability 2 exp(−cβ na2 ), for every fixed a > 0. A union over the fixed net makes
this estimate simultaneous at all its points.
    To pass from the net to every parameter, use the quadratic form in Equation (D.22).
Whenever ∥W ∥ and the empirical field second moment are bounded, its operator norm is
at most a fixed Kform . Indeed, ∥W ∥ controls the random term, the coherent terms are
bounded rank forms, and the remaining kernels are bounded. The estimates above give
exponentially small probability for the complement of this event. For any residual directions,
changing (B, R) to (B ′ , R′ ) while keeping the same unit direction in each residual space
changes the vector p by exactly ∥(B, R) − (B ′ , R′ )∥2 . Hence the form changes by at most
2Kform ∥(B, R) − (B ′ , R′ )∥2 . If a residual norm is zero, choose any direction in its residual
space for this comparison. The event giving at least two sites per bin ensures that every such
space is nonzero for large n. Thus the maximum of the net bounds, enlarged by 2Kform η,
controls the supremum over all normalized parameters.

Proof of Proposition D.1. We assemble the estimates with five fixed error allowances. Given δ
from the hypothesis, put a = δ/10. First require an error at most a in Equation (D.17). Next
choose the field partition and tolerances to make each error in Equations (D.22) and (D.24)
at most a. Take concentration error a in Equation (D.30) and choose η so that 2Kform η ≤ a.
Every choice precedes the limit in n. The rescaling in Equation (D.23) makes the limiting
functions admissible for the hypothesis. Functional approximation and concentration therefore
bound each net value by 1 − δ + 2a. The two form errors and extension from the net add the
remaining 3a, giving, for every unit vector,
                                                          δ
                                 MJ (p) ≤ 1 − δ + 5a = 1 − .
                                                          2
   To verify that this simultaneous assertion retains an exponential rate, collect the failure
probabilities after fixing all parameters. Their sum has the form
                                                                               2
               Cn2 e−c1 n + 2nNw e−c2 n + Cke−c3 n + 2Npar e−c4 n + 2 9n e−c5 n ,
                               Nw < ∞,         Npar ≤ (1 + 2/η)2k .

Each ci is positive and depends only on β and the fixed choices. The initial terms account for
the entry, diagonal, norm, empirical-tail and common-shift events; Nw counts the deterministic
row-parameter mesh. Only the last term uses an exponentially large sphere net, and its
rate quadratic in n absorbs 9n . The other prefactors are fixed or polynomial in n. Hence
the sum is at most e−κn for some κ > 0 and all sufficiently large n. Finally the event in
Equation (D.6) does not involve the auxiliary diagonals, so its probability on the enlarged
space equals that on the planted marginal.




                                               132
D.6    From planted probability to Gibbs mass
The planted estimate controls a disorder event for one configuration. Sign conjugation turns
it into a bound on the expected unnormalized weight of all exceptional configurations. To
obtain Gibbs mass, we also need the partition function to remain near its annealed value on
the exponential scale.

Lemma D.4 (Annealed transfer). Fix 0 < β < 1. Let An be a measurable event for
symmetric zero-diagonal interaction matrices, and suppose that P  ˆ︁ n (An ) ≤ e−κn for all large
n, with a fixed κ > 0. For x ∈ {−1, 1}n , write J x = diag(x)J diag(x) and define

                                         BJ = {x : J x ∈ An }.

There are events whose original disorder probabilities tend to one on which

                                          µJ (BJ ) ≤ e−κn/2 .                                 (D.31)

In fact the complementary probabilities can be bounded by e−cn for a fixed c > 0.

Proof. Let ZJ be the original partition function and

E     A uniform bound on the variational expression
This appendix supplies the variational gap required by the planted comparison. We use
the notation of Section D: h ∼ N (β 2 , β 2 ), brackets denote expectation under this law, and
m = tanh h, v = 1 − m2 , w = 1 − m. The bound below has one numerical margin for the
entire stated temperature interval and for all admissible functions.
Proposition E.1 (Uniform scalar gap). For every β ∈ [0.35, 0.5], every real measurable
b ∈ L2 , and every measurable r ≥ 0 in L2 satisfying ⟨b2 + r2 ⟩ = 1, the expression in
Equation (D.5) satisfies
                                 Vβ (b, r) ≤ 0.9969.
    Every terminating decimal in the proof denotes an exact rational number. We will apply
Young’s inequality with a positive field-dependent multiplier to separate the square-root term
into quadratic terms in b and r. The multiplier has two jobs: the total diagonal coefficient
must stay below .9789, and its cost on b2 must absorb the remaining quadratic form, with an
excess of at most .018⟨b2 ⟩. Normalization will then give .9789 + .018 = .9969. The functional
reductions are proved here. The scalar enclosures on which they rely, including quadrature
and arithmetic errors, are proved in Appendix H.

E.1    Identities and the positive kernel remainder
We begin with two identities that simplify the scalar coefficients. Conditioned on |h| = t, the
sign of the field has weights proportional to et and e−t , and hence mean tanh t. Multiplying
by an even function of the field gives, for every q for which the expectations exist,
                                      ⟨mq(m2 )⟩ = ⟨m2 q(m2 )⟩.                                    (E.1)
In particular, ⟨m⟩ = ⟨m2 ⟩ and ⟨mv⟩ = ⟨m2 v⟩. The second identity follows by Gaussian
integration by parts with v ′ = −2mv:
                    Cov(h, v) = −2β 2 ⟨mv⟩,              ⟨(h − β 2 + 2β 2 m)v⟩ = 0.               (E.2)
   Choose the following positive multiplier for Young’s inequality. Its associated cost k will

bounds justify the divisions below. To bound the kernel term, define

Proof of Proposition E.1. We can now assemble the two bounds. Insert the coherent-form
estimate of Lemma E.5 into Equation (E.11), and then apply the diagonal envelope of
Lemma E.4:

          Vβ (b, r) ≤ ⟨(D + k(m) + Λ)(b2 + r2 )⟩ + .018⟨b2 ⟩ ≤ .9789 + .018 = .9969.

The argument applies to the full function class in the proposition. The coefficients h, hv
belong to L2 , all other coefficient functions are bounded, and Lemma E.2 already justifies the
kernel integral for signed, unbounded b. The quantity under the last radical in Equation (D.5)
is at most 4⟨b2 + r2 ⟩ = 4, making its pairing with r finite. Thus Fubini for the nonnegative
quadratic integrals and every use of Cauchy–Schwarz are valid without an infinity-norm
bound on either function. The estimate consequently also covers concentration on arbitrarily
rare field sets.


F    Avoiding exceptional states and the mixing scale
The gradient estimate contracts outside an exceptional set of small Gibbs mass. To use it
from an arbitrary initial configuration, we must also control the time the chain spends in
that set. We first obtain this control after a constant burn-in, then propagate gradients over
logarithmic times. The resulting concentration bounds will locate the median mixing time on
that same scale.
    Fix β ∈ [0, 1/2). Intersect the good-disorder events in Propositions B.2, B.4, B.5, and C.2,
and denote the resulting event by Gn . Then PJ (Gn ) → 1. We collect the inputs on this event
before starting the argument. There are constants K, CE , CL , γ, κ > 0 and A ≥ 0, depending
only on the fixed β, such that the following bounds hold for all sufficiently large n and every
realization in Gn . The interaction satisfies ∥J∥ ≤ K, the continuous-time gap is at least γ,
and the transition density has the entropy bound
                                 St (x, ·)
                            (︃               )︃
                     Entµ                         ≤ CE ne−t/CL        (x ∈ Ωn , t ≥ 0).             (F.1)
                                     µ
Here the quotient denotes the density with respect to µ. There is also a set B = B(J) ⊆ Ωn
such that

             µ(B) ≤ e−κn ,
                                                                (︁            )︁
                                     (∂r − L)U (Sr f ) ≤ −κ + A1B U (Sr f )               (r ≥ 0)   (F.2)

                                                         139
for every real function f on Ωn . The same κ can be used for the exceptional mass and the
contraction rate by shrinking it and enlarging A. From now on we work deterministically
on Gn : both the constants and the sufficiently large dimension thresholds are uniform over
this event. Suppressing the fixed disorder, we write Px , Ex for the law and expectation of the
continuous-time chain started at x.

F.1    Gradient propagation and jump concentration
We begin by expressing the gradient bound along a chain trajectory. The exponential weight
below records contraction outside the exceptional set and the possible loss during visits to it.
A rough bound on this weight will suffice for the initial burn-in.
Lemma F.1 (Feynman–Kac comparison). Put V = −κ + A1B . For every real f , every
0 ≤ s ≤ d, and every initial configuration x,

                    U (Sd f )(x) ≤ Ex exp              V (Xu ) du U (Sd−s f )(Xs ) .        (F.3)
                                                 0

Consequently,
                            ∥U (Sr f )∥∞ ≤ eAr ∥U (f )∥∞                (r ≥ 0).            (F.4)
Proof. Let G(r, x) = U (Sr f )(x). Equation (F.2) gives −∂r G + LG + V G ≥ 0. Following the
chain forward while running the semigroup parameter backward therefore gives nonnegative
drift for                (︃∫︂ u           )︃
                     exp        V (Xv ) dv G(d − u, Xu ),    0 ≤ u ≤ s,
                                 0
so the finite state space and bounded time interval justify taking expectations. This proves
Equation (F.3). For the rough envelope, take s = d = r and bound the exponential weight
using V ≤ A. This gives Equation (F.4).

    To convert gradient envelopes into fluctuation bounds we use Lemma 2.2, with U (f ) =
∥df ∥2 . Its complete finite-chain proof uses only the heat-bath jump rates, so it applies
throughout the present argument without a temperature assumption.

F.2    Uniform avoidance after a constant burn-in
Small Gibbs mass alone does not control a chain started inside the exceptional set. We first
show that entropy dissipation moves a fixed amount of probability a macroscopic Hamming
distance away from that set. Concentration of the distance then makes the probability of a
visit at any later deterministic time very small.
Proposition F.2. There are constants d0 , ca > 0, independent of n and the realization in

F.3     Linear observables and the order of the mixing time
The propagated gradient has a bounded time integral for every linear observable with bounded
coefficient norm. The jump exponential-moment estimate therefore gives fluctuations of
bounded size, uniformly over all such observables and all starts. We will use this uniformity
to choose a distinguishing observable after the disorder and the initial configuration have
been selected.

G     Cutoff and the discrete update clock
Section F placed the median mixing time on a logarithmic scale. We now show that, once the
chain has a fixed positive amount of common mass with equilibrium, any additional positive
logarithmic lag makes its total-variation distance vanish. Applying this implication on either
side of the median proves continuous-time cutoff; a comparison of update clocks then gives
the discrete-time result.
    Fix β ∈ [0, 1/2). Throughout this section, the disorder belongs to the simultaneous good
events Gn from Section F. In particular, ∥J∥ ≤ K, the spectral gap is at least γ > 0, and the
constants in Equation (F.8) are uniform on these events. We use its notation κ > 0, Cdyn ,
and rn . For each fixed D∗ > 0, Equation (F.8) applies whenever 0 ≤ s ≤ d ≤ D∗ log n, and
its deterministic remainder satisfies

                            np rn −→ 0         for every fixed p > 0.                    (G.1)

We retain this uniformity throughout: unless stated otherwise, every limit is uniform over Gn .
Constants may depend on the fixed inverse temperature and on the fixed parameters in the
relevant statement.

G.1    Common mass and local fields
If two laws share a fixed positive amount of mass, their means can be separated only to the
extent permitted by their variances. The following elementary estimate makes this precise.
We first apply it to local fields; later we apply it to bounded test functions after semigroup
smoothing.

Lemma G.1 (Means under measures with common mass). Let ν and π be probability
measures on a finite set, and suppose ∥ν − π∥TV ≤ 1 − ε, where ε > 0. For every real function
H on that set,                             √          √
                                             Varν H + Varπ H
                             |νH − πH| ≤           √            .                       (G.2)
                                                     ε




                                                144
Proof. Take the common part of the measures, ρ(x) = min{ν(x), π(x)}, whose mass is
q = 1 − ∥ν − π∥TV ≥ ε. We compare each mean to the mean under the normalized common
part. Since ρ ≤ ν, Cauchy–Schwarz gives
                      ⃓        ⃓   ⃓                  ⃓ √︄
                      ⃓     ρH ⃓ 1 ⃓∑︂                ⃓    Varν H
                      ⃓νH −    ⃓= ⃓    ρ(x)(H(x) − νH)⃓ ≤         .
                                   ⃓                  ⃓
                      ⃓      q ⃓ q⃓        x
                                                      ⃓      q
The same calculation for π bounds the other difference from the common mean. The triangle
inequality, followed by the lower bound on the common mass, proves the assertion.

    Write hi (x) = (Jx)i for the local field. Its coefficient vector has Euclidean norm at most
K. Spin inversion gives µhi = 0 in the zero-field model. Thus the concentration estimate for
linear observables applies to every local field. Common mass will also control its transient
mean, yielding the following tail bound at each deterministic observation time.
Lemma G.2 (Fields at deterministic observation times). Fix ε ∈ (0, 1), δ > 0, and D∗ > 0.
Suppose dc (t) ≤ 1 − ε. There is a deterministic sequence τn = τn (ε, δ, D∗ ) such that

from any point x has common mass at least ε with µ for every z ≥ t. Proposition F.4 bounds
Varνx,z hi uniformly in i, x, z, and the equilibrium variance is at most K 2 /γ. Lemma G.1
therefore gives a constant Bε such that
                              |Ex hi (Xz )| ≤ Bε     (t ≤ z ≤ D∗ log n).                  (G.4)

                             [︃                           ]︃

For a prescribed p > 0, first fix θ with θδ > p + 1, and then let n tend to infinity. Define
τn as the supremum of the displayed probabilities over the indicated parameters and good
disorders. The same sequence therefore has every asserted inverse-power decay rate. The
uniformity is over deterministic times. In the smoothing argument we will integrate the
resulting expectation bounds, so a simultaneous field event along the whole sample path is
unnecessary.

G.2    A positive logarithmic lag finishes mixing
The passage from a nontrivial total-variation overlap to mixing after a positive logarithmic lag
is related to the concentration-based cutoff mechanism of Salez [23, Theorem 5 and Lemma 9].
Here we prove the required implication through variances of bounded smoothed tests and
their common mass with equilibrium.
    We now prove the implication needed for cutoff. The common-mass hypothesis will
compare expectations of a smoothed test once its variance is small under both the transient
law and equilibrium. The main task is the transient variance: gradient propagation will
bound the martingale energy before the observation time by the energy in a later interval of
positive logarithmic length.

                                                   145
Proposition G.3 (Positive-lag smoothing). Fix ε ∈ (0, 1), α > 0, and D∗ > 0, and put
s = α log n. Uniformly over all times t ≥ 0 satisfying

                                 dc (t) ≤ 1 − ε,          t + s ≤ D∗ log n,

one has dc (t + s) → 0.

    Positive-lag smoothing turns the logarithmic median scale into cutoff. At the median
there is enough common mass to apply the proposition. If such common mass appeared a
fixed fraction earlier, the same proposition would force mixing before the median, which is
impossible.

H     Certificate for the scalar enclosures
The scalar argument needs certified moments of the field law N (β 2 , β 2 ), with β ∈ [7/20, 1/2].
Retain the notation of Section E. We now prove Lemma E.3 and verify the arithmetic in
Table 2.
    There are three distinct error estimates. First, a complex-strip bound controls replacement
of each integral by a finite quadrature sum. Second, directed integer arithmetic encloses
those sums, which use 41 nodes at each of five exact temperatures. Third, normalization,
standard deviations, and the two mean parameters in F must be transferred back to the
continuous field law. The estimates below keep these steps separate. The interpolation
between temperatures remains the analytic argument of Section E.
Lemma H.1 (Whole-line trapezoidal estimate). Let t > 0. Suppose f is continuous on
{z : |ℑz| ≤ t}, holomorphic in its interior, and satisfies a bound

any mesh ∆ > 0,          ⃓                          ⃓

Proof. We compare the integral and the infinite quadrature sum through the Fourier coeffi-
                                                       ∑︁
cients of the periodization. The assumed decay  makes k f (x + k∆) uniformly convergent on
a period. Its coefficient at integer j is ∆−1 R f (x)e−2πijx/∆ dx. To estimate a nonconstant
                                             ∫︁

coefficient, shift the contour to height −t when j > 0, and to height t when j < 0. The
integrals on the vertical sides vanish by the uniform Gaussian decay. One may first shift
to an interior line and then pass to the boundary by dominated convergence, so the stated
boundary regularity suffices. The resulting bound for the absolute value of the coefficient
is (M/∆)e−2πt|j|/∆ . These bounds are summable. To identify the Fourier series with the
continuous periodization without a further regularity assumption, take arithmetic means
of the partial Fourier sums. Relative to normalized measure on a period, their kernels are
      ∑︁ −1 2πijx/∆ 2
N −1 | N j=0 e        | . The kernels are nonnegative, have integral one, and concentrate at
the origin. Their convolutions therefore converge uniformly to the continuous periodization.
Absolute convergence identifies this limit with the Fourier series itself. At zero, the constant
coefficient is the integral divided by ∆; summing the bounds for the remaining coefficients
proves Equation (H.1).

Directed integer arithmetic. We now give the finite recipe that establishes the enclosures
just listed. It requires no accuracy assumption for a transcendental library: exponentials and
square roots are enclosed using integer arithmetic and explicit remainders. Fix S = 1050 , and
represent an interval by integer endpoints [u, v], meaning [u/S, v/S]. Represent a rational
input by its lower and upper nearest multiples of S −1 . Addition and subtraction act on
endpoints in the usual way. For multiplication, take the minimum and maximum of the four
integer endpoint products, then divide by S, rounding the minimum down and the maximum
up. For an interval not containing zero, its reciprocal is represented by
                                                  [︄⌊︄        ⌋︄ ⌈︄      ⌉︄]︄
                                                         S2   S2
                                                            ,                   .
                                                         v    u

The reciprocal rule applies to positive and negative intervals alike; division is multiplication
                                                                                   √        √ by
this reciprocal. For a nonnegative interval, square roots are represented by [⌊ uS⌋, ⌈ vS⌉],
computed by integer square root and an exact square comparison. Powers use repeated
interval multiplication. Every operation preserves inclusion.

I   A finite-chain clock comparison
This appendix proves the bounded-array clock lemma stated in Section 2. Both cutoff
arguments use it to pass from shifted Poisson averages to a binomial average after extracting
a fixed holding probability from the update kernel. The parameters and arrays below are
those of Lemma 2.3; the proof uses no property of the SK model. It follows the shifted-
clock comparison of Chen and Saloff-Coste [9, Theorem 1.1 and Lemmas 3.1–3.2], with the
finite approximation fixed before the arrays are tested. The Gaussian approximation below
uses signed coefficients: cancellation permits translates of the wider Poisson Gaussian to
approximate the narrower binomial Gaussian.


                                                   154
Proof of Lemma 2.3. The proof has three steps. We approximate the two rescaled clocks
in L1 by Gaussian densities, approximate the narrower Gaussian by a finite signed sum
of translates of the wider one, and then test this single finite approximation against the
bounded arrays. Fixing that approximation before taking the limit will also give the uniform
statement.
    Step 1: the two local limits. For an integer M and j ∈ Z, let
                                     j − qM j + 1 − qM
                                        [︃                        )︃
                              IM,j =   √   ,   √                       .
                                         M        M
                                                                    √
The histogram density of an integer-valued random √   variable Z is M P(Z = j) on IM,j .
Denote by pM,y the histogram for Poisson(qM + y M ) and by qM the histogram for
Binomial(M, q), assigning zero probability outside their supports. For v > 0 write
                                                1      2
                                   φv (x) = √       e−x /(2v) .
                                                2πv
We claim that for every fixed y,

                   ∥pM,y − φq ( · − y)∥1 −→ 0,        ∥qM − φq(1−q) ∥1 −→ 0.               (I.1)

    Here is a proof from probability ratios. Let πM (j) denote the probability mass function
of either variable, let kM be an integer nearest its mean, and put v = q in the Poisson case
and v = q(1 − q) in the binomial case. Their successive ratios are, respectively,

                       m              (M − j)q                    √

                      j+1          (j + 1)(1 − q)

For fixed H this bounds the limit inferior and limit superior of AM by (1 − v/H 2 )/JH and

uniform convergence of the histogram densities on every compact interval, with limiting center
y in the Poisson case and zero in the binomial case. The same Chebyshev estimate controls

                                               155
their tails; the histogram intervals move an observation by at most M −1/2 in the rescaled
coordinate. Gaussian tails then give the whole-line L1 (R) convergence in Equation (I.1).
    Step 2: approximation by translates. We claim that the narrower Gaussian belongs to the
                                                                                     (k)
closed linear span, in L1 , of translates of the wider Gaussian. Every derivative φq belongs
to that closed span: its finite differences are finite linear combinations of translates, and
their quotients converge to the derivative in L1 . For completeness, iterating the Fundamental
Theorem of Calculus expresses the kth forward-difference quotient as
                          ∫︂
                                      φ(k)
                                       q (x + h(t1 + · · · + tk )) dt1 · · · dtk .
                            [0,1]k

Gaussian derivatives are integrable polynomial multiples of a Gaussian, and their translations
are continuous in L1 by dominated convergence. This proves the asserted limit. Repeated
                             (k)
differentiation also gives φq = pk φq , where pk is a polynomial of degree k with leading
coefficient (−1/q)k . The nonzero leading coefficients therefore let us express every polynomial
multiple of φq as a finite linear combination of these derivatives. Such multiples lie in the
same closed span.
    Put v = q and w = q(1 − q). The ratio of the two densities is
                                        √︃
                        φw (x)               v −cx2               1        1   1
                                                                      (︃           )︃
                               =               e    ,          c=            −          > 0.
                        φv (x)               w                    2        w v
                                                                    2
If V is centered Gaussian of variance 2c, then e−cx = E cos(V x). This identity follows
directly from Gaussian integration by parts: the characteristic function has value one at
zero and derivative −2cx times itself. Truncate the expectation to |V | ≤ R. The omitted
contribution has absolute value at most P(|V | > R), uniformly in x. For fixed R, replace
cosine by its Taylor polynomials. On |V | ≤ R, their absolute values are bounded by eR|x| ,
which is integrable against φv (x) dx. Dominated convergence thus approximates the truncated
expectation by polynomials in L1 (φv (x) dx). Sending R to infinity and using the preceding
derivative argument proves the claimed closed-span property for φw .
    Step 3: test one finite approximation against the arrays. Extend an,j by zero to j < 0,
and let Un be the function equal to an,j on IMn ,j . If B = 0 the result is immediate, so assume
B > 0. Given ϵ∗ > 0, choose one finite signed sum

                                       ℓ=1

For large n, the first L1 error is below ϵ∗ /(4B), each of the finitely many other L1 errors is
below ϵ∗ /(4BA0 ), and each of the Poisson averages is below ϵ∗ /(4A0 ). The right side is then
at most ϵ∗ . This proves the result. The same bound proves its uniform version: the finite
approximation is common to the family, the local-limit errors depend only on M and the
fixed shifts, and the hypothesis controls the finite list of Poisson averages uniformly.
